DOI: 10.1111/1475-679X.12606

Journal of Accounting Research

Vol. 63 No. 4 September 2025

Printed in U.S.A.

CHICAGO BOOTH

# Accounting Information Usage and Trading by Retail Investors: Evidence from Integrated Trading Platform

JACKY CHAU $ ^{*} $

Received 1 August 2021; accepted 14 October 2024

## ABSTRACT

This registered report investigates self-directed retail investors' information choices and trading decisions on an integrated trading platform that provides timely and convenient access to accounting information. The analyses reveal that these investors access a mosaic of information, with a high proportion not firm-specific. In accessing accounting disclosures, retail investors are more interested in media articles providing summaries or expert analyses than original filings. Retail trades on this integrated trading platform are

more informed than another retail trading platform providing little information services while exhibiting no significant differences in informativeness compared with institutional trades using Bulge Bracket platforms. In particular, trade informativeness is more pronounced when there are accounting disclosures. The evidence suggests that self-directed retail investors can benefit from a trading environment that provides rich and convenient access to a mosaic of information, particularly timely accounting disclosures.

JEL codes: G11, G12, M40, M41

Keywords: retail investor; online brokerage; information usage; accounting; trading

## 1. Introduction

Retail investors can be significant market participants in emerging markets. Recent press articles suggest that retail investors are on the rise and may no longer be a sideshow to the market even in developed markets such as the United States. $ ^{1} $ The proliferation of low-cost digital trading platforms has enabled retailer investors to directly trade stocks with less friction—lower trading and information processing costs. It is unclear how investors' information choice manifests with new information technologies that reduce certain information processing costs (e.g., Blankespoor, deHaan, and Marinovic [2020]). As such, this registered report examines the emerging class of self-directed online retail investors $ ^{2} $ and seeks to understand how they utilize information, particularly accounting information, in their stock trading decisions.

Prior literature finds that retail investors appear to neglect value-relevant accounting information (e.g., Hirshleifer et al. [2008]; Blankespoor et al. [2019]). Early evidence often suggests that retail investors are likely unsophisticated noise traders who underperform relative to the market (e.g., Kumar and Lee [2006]; Hvidkjaer [2008]; Barber et al. [2009]). Other research suggests that retail investor trades could be informative and predictive of stock returns (e.g., Kaniel et al. [2012]; Kelley and Tetlock [2013, 2016]); Boehmer et al. [2021]. It is not clear if the mixed evidence is due to the nonhomogenous nature of retail investors or noisy measurements of retail investors' activities, e.g., using trade size to infer investor types.

This registered report takes advantage of a unique setting in which the researchers can observe both the aggregate information usage and trades of retail investors at the firm level in an integrated trading platform. Besides offering trading services, an integrated trading platform serves as an information platform for investors. The study utilizes data from an integrated

platform named FutuBull. As an emerging low-cost online and mobile stock-broker headquartered in Hong Kong (HK), FutuBull has grown rapidly and become the leading retail broker in terms of trading volume in 2021, with its clients holding a total of 170 billion HKD worth of stocks. With rich financial information integrated, the platform has the potential to dramatically lower the information processing costs for retail investors.

Two features make the setting special for the study. First, the FutuBull platform provides timely news feeds on filings and information intermediaries, with views tracked for each document. This enables researchers to trace the cumulative views on each filing or media article. While prior research examines specific channels of information (e.g., robo-journalism, analyst information, Edgar filings), the setting allows for a holistic evaluation of their relative importance under the same platform. $ ^{3} $ Second, the daily broker-level holdings are publicly available for the HK stock markets, providing a unique opportunity to simultaneously observe both the information usage patterns and the aggregate holdings of all FutuBull traders by firms on each trading day. $ ^{4} $

The study begins by examining the information access patterns of over one million documents that appear in the corporate news feeds of 2579 HK-listed firms on the FutuBull platform from July 2014 to June 2021. Regulatory filings represent 37.3% of all documents, while media articles represent the rest 62.7%. However, filings contribute to only 2.8% of total cumulative views among these documents, suggesting that self-directed investors generally display a lower interest in accessing regulatory filings relative to media articles.

Firm-specific articles contribute to only 21.6% of total cumulative views despite its 62.3% dominance in quantity among media articles. While only 1.4% of articles are tagged under thematic topics, they contribute to 22.7% of total cumulative views. These statistics suggest that self-directed retail investors rely on a mosaic of information other than firm-specific information. Investor attention appears to be the key determinant for information access on the platform. 3.3% of articles promoted in the headline contribute to 62.4% of total cumulative views of media articles. This suggests

that trading platforms may exert strong influences on users' information access. Among firm-specific articles, retail investors display a higher interest in accessing those covering accounting information, suggesting that retail investors regard accounting information as an important source of firm-specific information. The results also find that retail investors display a higher interest in accessing articles providing summary reports or expert analyses over the disclosures in original filings during earnings announcements.

Next, the study examines whether and how self-directed retail investors integrate accounting information into trading decisions. When access to Results Announcements filings is low, their aggregate retail trades are generally in the opposite direction of earnings news. But if the access is high, their trades are more in line with earnings news. In the broader sample of accounting disclosures, the aggregate trading decisions by these retail investors at the time of disclosures are significantly negatively associated with disclosure news, resembling the behavior of contrarian traders. The aggregate trades before disclosures appear to exhibit consistent direction with upcoming news, albeit statistically insignificant. This suggests that some retail investors may attempt to profit from anticipating the news contained in upcoming disclosures. Along with the evidence on information access, the findings support the presence of diverse trading strategies among self-directed retail investors.

Finally, the study investigates the informativeness of retail investor trades at FutuBull. Following Kelly and Tetlock [2016], the study uses the daily Fama and MacBeth [1973] regression and finds that the aggregate retail trades at FutuBull are generally predictive of future abnormal returns over the next 20 days. The informativeness is even more pronounced in the short window after accounting disclosures, consistent with the prevalence of informed retail trading based on processing accounting information at FutuBull. The informativeness of retail trades at FutuBull, is significantly higher than I-Access, a no-frills trading platform that provides virtually no information services, and exhibits no significant differences compared with institutional trades at Bulge Bracket brokers. Similar findings are obtained when the study calculates the trading profitability around accounting disclosures among those that exhibit clear trading directions by different brokerage users assuming a three-month or six-month horizon. These findings suggest that self-directed retail investors significantly benefit from having convenient access to timely accounting information in their trading platforms.

This registered report contributes to the literature on information acquisition activities of self-directed retail investors. Technological advancements that drastically lower trading and information processing costs have the potential to empower a growing class of self-directed retail investors. Prior literature has examined information acquisition through different channels largely in isolation, such as SEC Edgar filings (e.g., Drake, Roulstone, and Thornock [2015]), and robo-journalism (e.g., Blankespoor, deHaan,

and Zhu [2018]). However, the relative contribution by different channels is not clear. Using the setting of an integrated trading platform, this registered report provides a more holistic assessment of retail investors' information preference over various information sources and types under a unified viewership scale. In particular, the study shows that self-directed retail investors access a mosaic of information, with a significant portion devoted to articles covering information that is not firm-specific. In this setting, retail investors generally display a higher interest in accessing crowdsourced content over traditional sell-side research information as well as a lower interest in viewing robo-journalism articles.

The study also contributes to the literature on retail investors' use of accounting information. While Blankespoor et al. [2019] find that the retail investors in their sample neglect value-relevant accounting information presented in articles in trading decisions, other studies (e.g., Kaniel et al. [2012]) find some evidence of informed trading by retail investors around earnings announcements. The mixed prior evidence may suggest that retail investors are likely not a homogenous group of investors. The study utilizes a setting with low acquisition cost on accounting information to elicit how self-directed retail investors access timely accounting information. The results show that retail investors are more interested in media summaries or expert analyses relative to original filings in accessing timely accounting disclosures. Thus, the study helps elucidate the presence of significant friction in using accounting information from regulatory filings by self-directed retail investors.

Further, the study provides additional insights regarding whether self-directed retail investors properly integrate accounting information in trading decisions. Consistent with some recent literature suggesting that retail trades can be information-driven (e.g., Kelley and Tetlock [2013, 2016]; Boehmer et al. [2021]), the study finds that the aggregate trading decisions by self-directed retail investors at FutuBull are generally positively predictive of future returns. The informativeness is more pronounced after accounting disclosures, consistent with these investors being able to properly integrate accounting information in trading decisions. The study also finds that the trading decisions by these retail investors vary depending on the level of information access to Results Announcement filings during earnings announcements. On the other hand, the aggregate retail trades at I-Access are generally negatively predictive of future returns. The evidence suggests that self-directed retail investors can be quite heterogeneous in their trade informativeness and strategies, which vary by their trading environment or information access.

Last but not least, this registered report should be first in documenting the information role of trading platforms for retail investors. The results show that retail investors at FutuBull are significantly more informed than those at I-Access, suggesting that retail investors using an integrated trading platform with much lower information processing costs can better incorporate accounting information in their trading decisions. The results also

find that aggregate retail trades at FutuBull are as informed as aggregate institutional trades at Bulge Bracket brokers, suggesting that an integrated trading environment with convenient access to a rich set of information can significantly narrow the information gap between self-directed retail investors and institutional investors. The findings should be relevant to regulators who are showing more interest in understanding self-directed retail investors, especially the role of brokers in their trading behaviors.

## 2. Background and Hypotheses

### 2.1 INFORMATION USAGE BY RETAIL INVESTORS

Prior archival studies mostly rely on either small trades or proprietary brokerage trading data to identify retail investors. However, it is difficult to get a complete picture of retail investors' information usage patterns on a large scale as they may obtain information from a variety of sources and the entire process is often not observable. While recent studies (e.g., Ben-Rephael, Da, and Israelsen [2017]) have utilized the data from Bloomberg terminals to examine investors' information usage, the vast majority of Bloomberg terminal users are likely institutional investors. Other studies have focused on specific information sources, e.g., analysts' information (e.g., Lawrence, Ryans, and Sun [2017]), Edgar filings (e.g., Drake, Roulstone, and Thornock [2015]), newsflashes (e.g., Twedt [2015]), robojournalisms (e.g., Blankespoor et al. [2019]). However, the relative importance of different types of information to retail investors is still unknown. In particular, the evidence is somewhat mixed regarding what extent retail investors utilize accounting information, which is shown by the literature to be value-relevant and useful in predicting future returns (e.g., Ball and Brown [1968]; Bernard and Thomas [1989]; Sloan [1996]; Lewellen [2004]).

On the one hand, retail investors are sometimes regarded as “naïve” investors who are prone to behavioral biases such as overconfidence (e.g., Barber and Odean [2000]; Statman, Thorley, and Vorkink [2006]), the disposition effect (e.g., Shefrin and Statman [1985]; Odean [1998]) and attention-driven purchase (e.g., Barber and Odean [2007]). They also appear to be naïve about analyst incentives (Malmendier and Shanthikumar [2007]). Early evidence mostly suggests that retail investors are likely unsophisticated noise traders who may underperform relative to the market (e.g., Kumar and Lee [2006]; Hvidkjaer [2008]; Barber, Odean, and Zhu [2008]). They tend to rely on technical analysis in trading decisions (Grinblatt and Keloharju [2000], Barber and Odean [2008], Kaniel, Saar and Titman [2008]) but appear to ignore value-relevant earnings information (e.g., Blankespoor et al. [2019]). Blankespoor et al. [2019] formulate a sequential framework breaking down information usage costs into awareness cost, acquisition cost, and integration cost. Information usage

costs could impede retail investors from acquiring and utilizing accounting information.

On the other hand, an increasing proportion of the world's population has received business education and developed exposure to basic accounting knowledge over time. $ ^{5} $ Technological advancements also greatly reduce the cost of information usage and knowledge acquisition. $ ^{6} $ These developments could lower all three components of information usage costs for retail investors. Recent research presents some evidence that retail investor trades could be informative. For example, Kaniel et al. [2012] present the first evidence of informed trading by retail investors around earnings announcements. They show that pre-announcement trades by retail investors are predictive of returns on and after earnings announcement dates. Kelley and Tetlock [2013, 2016] find that the trades by retail investors are predictive of stock returns. Boehmer et al. [2021] suggest that retail marketable orders contain firm-level information that is not yet incorporated into prices.

Recent advances in digital technologies may empower a new class of self-directed retail investors. These investors may choose to forego the assistance of an investment dealer or advisor and conduct their own research and analysis in investment decisions. Since 2021, regulators and financial institutions have displayed rising interest in this emerging class of self-directed retail investors (e.g., Britainthinks [2021]; Deloitte [2021]; Dure [2021]). Through direct surveys, the Ontario Securities Commission [2021] finds that self-directed investors have diverse demographic backgrounds and trading experiences. $ ^{7} $

### 2.2 INTEGRATED TRADING PLATFORM

Self-directed retail investors are empowered by the rapid growth of low-cost self-directed digital trading platforms developed by new fintech startups or in the digital transformation of traditional brokerage houses. Several online brokers that serve the US or HK stock markets have switched to low-cost or even zero-commission operating models. The most famous example in the US setting is Robinhood, which provides a simple and

commission-free mobile-centric platform. $ ^{8} $ The review article by Blankespoor, deHaan, and Marinovic [2020] points out that an unresearched area is the effect of new trading platforms which reduce explicit trading costs of small investors to nearly zero.

In addition to drastically lowering trading costs, these new digital self-directed trading platforms can potentially reduce investors' costs of information processing. An unexplored research area is the role of self-directed trading platforms in facilitating information processing by retail investors. Not all self-directed trading platforms are created equal. Besides trading services, there can be large variations in the amount of financial information integrated into the platform. Some trading platforms offer comprehensive financial information for retail investors akin to that of Bloomberg while others integrate minimal accounting information. $ ^{9} $ Other brokerages, however, may provide different experiences through integrating more varieties of information. $ ^{10} $

By providing convenient and timely access to accounting information and news feeds in the trading interface, integrated trading platforms likely lower the information processing cost and have the potential to become a primary information acquisition channel for self-directed retail investors. Thus, the data in integrated trading platforms can potentially provide a more holistic picture of the information usage patterns of these groups of retail investors, whereas prior studies typically focus on one particular information channel in isolation.

### 2.3 PREDICTIONS AND HYPOTHESES

2.3.1. Information Access. Public firms are typically required to provide accounting disclosures in regulatory filings. Firms also often issue press releases providing financial highlights. Financial journalists often cover and summarize earnings news in press articles while some articles can be generated by bots (e.g., Blankespoor, deHaan, and Zhu [2018]) or bloggers. An extensive literature has documented the importance of the traditional media in the capital market (e.g., Bushee et al. [2010]).

Retail investors on integrated trading platforms have convenient access to a variety of accounting disclosures ranging from filings to media articles. Unlike institutional investors, retail investors may afford only limited time and resources in investing activities and are potentially more sensitive to the costs of information usage. If retail investors' information access pattern generally follows cost-benefit tradeoffs, the interest displayed by retail investors toward accounting information should increase with lower costs of information usage (e.g., Bloomfield [2002]; Blankespoor et al. [2019]). Therefore, their number of views on a specific document regarding accounting information would likely increase as the awareness costs or the expected acquisition/integration costs decrease.

As filings are often long with low readability (e.g., Dyer, Lang, and Stice-Lawrence [2017]), retail investors may require a much longer time to extract the relevant information. Retail investors could thus perceive higher expected information extraction costs from filings. Press articles are typically much shorter, offering succinct summaries of earnings news. If information acquisition cost is an important consideration, retail investors may prefer reading the summary highlights in media articles before deciding to delve into the more detailed disclosures in regulatory filings.

Lacking the expertise to analyze detailed financial statements, some retail investors may face high integration costs when directly utilizing the vast amount of information disclosed in regulatory filings. Thus, they could be particularly interested in soliciting expert opinions from professionals (e.g., sell-side analysts) to assist their interpretation of the disclosed accounting information. Consistent with this, Lawrence, Ryans, and Sun  $ [2017] $ find that investors have a higher demand for analyst information on Yahoo Finance during guidance and earnings announcements relative to the average trading day. If information integration cost is an important consideration, retail investors may prefer to rely more on expert opinions instead of directly conducting independent analyses using accounting information disclosed in filings.

Accounting information can be released through several types of disclosures, such as guidance, earnings announcements, and periodic financial reports. Retail investors may exhibit differential interest across different types of accounting disclosures depending on the cost–benefit tradeoffs, e.g., information extraction/integration costs, information timeliness, and information precision. Furthermore, whether articles providing news

summaries or expert opinions can substitute or complement retail investors' access to disclosures in regulatory filings is not clear. Press coverage may increase retail investors' awareness of disclosures and prompt the more sophisticated ones to seek more detailed information from filings after reading the summary reports or expert opinions. However, the less sophisticated investors may directly utilize the information from press articles instead of delving into detailed corporate filings.

Figure 1 provides a set of exploratory predictions regarding the information access patterns of self-directed retail investors based on the cost-benefit tradeoffs discussed.

2.3.2. Trading on Accounting Information. Some prior studies (e.g., Blankespoor et al. [2019]) suggest that retail investors may ignore or fail to integrate value-relevant accounting information into their trading decisions due to high processing costs. Further, retail investors may not necessarily have the requisite expertise to make informed trading decisions based on the information, even if accounting information can be easily acquired. With limited processing capacity (Sims [2003, 2010], these retail investors may rationally ignore accounting information if they cannot properly analyze and integrate it into trading decisions after cost-benefit analyses (e.g., Veldkamp [2011]). Instead, these retail investors may rely on other sources of information in which they are less disadvantaged in processing. Combining these prior findings and the rational inattention argument, the first

hypothesis in null form  $ (HI_{0}) $ is stated below if self-directed retail investors in integrated trading platforms are similar to those documented in prior settings (e.g., Blankespoor et al. [2019]).

 $ HI_{0} $: Self-directed retail investors using integrated trading platforms do not incorporate accounting information into their trading decisions around earnings news.

The null form of  $ H_{1} $ can be rejected if the self-directed retail investors in integrated trading platforms face a much lower cost of processing accounting information and there is a sufficiently large group of self-directed retail investors who have some expertise in processing accounting information. Through the incorporation of fundamental information and news feeds, integrated trading platforms have the potential to lower the costs of processing accounting information for self-directed retail investors. If high processing costs are the major impediments to retail investors' usage of accounting information, integrated trading platforms may increase retail investors' propensity to incorporate accounting information into trading decisions. Recent research also presents some evidence that retail investor trades could be informative (e.g., Kaniel et al. [2012]; Kelley and Tetlock [2013, 2016]; Boehmer et al. [2021]), suggesting the presence of some sophisticated retail investors.

However, it is unclear how retail investors should systematically incorporate accounting information in their trading decisions. One potential avenue is the post-earnings announcement drifts (PEADs) (e.g., Bernard and Thomas [1990]). To take advantage of PEADs, retail investors should trade in a direction in line with earnings surprises during earnings announcements. However, excess returns of PEADs generally hold at the portfolio level. Due to retail investors' limited portfolio size, it is likely costly for an individual to replicate the PEAD strategies following academic studies. $ ^{11} $ Alternatively, retail investors may profit from accounting information by predicting news in their announcements. Kaniel et al. [2012] show that preannouncement trades by retail investors are predictive of returns on and after earnings announcement dates. These retail investors may trade stocks in anticipation of earnings news and unwind the positions as the news is released. With limited financial resources, they may move on to the next opportunities instead of waiting for PEADs to play out over time. Under this strategy, these retail investors may resemble “contrarian” traders just after earnings announcements.

2.3.3. Trading Performance Using Accounting Information. Prior research suggests that retail investors often process accounting information

differently from professional investors who are more sophisticated. $ ^{12} $ Thus, retail investors in aggregate can still be trading against more informed professional traders and suffer from worse trading profitability if they either ultimately ignore or fail to integrate value-relevant accounting information into their trading decisions. If most self-directed retail investors lack the knowledge or expertise, these retail investors may simply ignore value-relevant accounting information in their trading decisions even though such information is relatively much easier to acquire through the integrated trading platform. Following the bounded rationality argument (e.g., Sims [2003, 2010]), the second hypothesis in null form  $ (H2_0) $ is stated below if self-directed retail investors in integrated trading platforms do not significantly benefit from having more access or convenient access to value-relevant accounting disclosures due to their limited processing capacity vs. institutional investors who may integrate and react to accounting information with much better precision and speed.

 $ H_{2} $0: Self-directed retail investors do not conduct more profitable trades around earnings news when they have more (convenient) access to value-relevant accounting disclosures.

However, if there is a sufficiently large group of more sophisticated self-directed retail investors who can manage to properly integrate value-relevant accounting information into trading decisions, they may narrow their disadvantages against institutional investors with the use of integrated trading platforms that offer low costs of information access. To the extent that accounting information is value-relevant, these retail investors may profit from trading using accounting information. For example, some may find it easier to process earnings news to predict future performance while others may better anticipate earnings news from prior disclosures or the mosaic of information easily accessed through the integrated trading platforms. If accounting information is value-relevant and integrated trading platforms lower the information processing costs for retail investors, self-directed retail investors that utilize integrated trading platforms may conduct more profitable trades than those that do not use integrated trading platforms.

## 3. Research Setting

### 3.1 REGISTERED REPORT PROCESS

This study was conducted as a Registered Report, a format designed to enhance the transparency, rigor, and reproducibility of academic research. The study was preregistered prior to data collection and analysis. From the conference version, the study benefited from the insightful and constructive feedback on the hypotheses and analytical plan. Based on the review process, the revised registered proposal refined the hypotheses and incorporated additional analyses, e.g., current tables 6 and 10. Data collection and analysis were subsequently conducted based on the final revised registered proposal that received conditional acceptance. The execution was strictly based on what was outlined in the registered proposal, except for some minor changes due to technical problems (e.g., small denominator problem) as outlined in appendix F.

### 3.2 INSTITUTIONAL FEATURES

This registered report utilizes the data from an integrated trading platform named FutuBull from HK. $ ^{13} $ Similar to the United States, there has been an emergence of discount online brokers in HK in the 2010s. Founded in 2011, FutuBull is a digitized brokerage platform that provides low-cost trading services and rich financial information for retail investors. Its holding company is listed on NASDAQ with ticker FUTU and has 1.24 million paying clients as of December 31, 2021. It is the largest retail broker by trading volume on the Hong Kong Stock Exchange (HKEX). In 2021, its clients collectively held 170 billion worth of stock positions in the HKEX, equivalent to about 1.3% shares for an average firm. The median client age was 37 in 2021, with 34% working in the internet, information technology, or financial services industries in 2020.

FutuBull's integrated trading platform provides investors with ready access to a variety of accounting-related information. FutuBull provides detailed historical accounting items within its platform, along with handy visualization tools for trend and margin analyses. More importantly, FutuBull provides timely news feeds covering all filings and articles from a multitude of information intermediaries (e.g., financial news outlets, sell-side research brokers, industry research firms, etc.) under a unified interface for retail investors, with views tracked for each document. This enables researchers to trace the cumulative views on each filing or article.

The information provided on FutuBull trading platform is highly comprehensive, aggregating the news from almost all free public sources available. Its users spent 38 minutes daily on average on the platform in 2020. As these are retail investors mostly with full-time jobs, a significant portion of their information acquisition activities likely happen within the platform.

Therefore, using data from the platform is particularly useful in painting a holistic picture regarding retail investors' information choices. Additionally, all relevant filings and articles appear under the same corporate "News" tabs and should be associated with similar information awareness/access costs for retail investors once they open the app interface for each company. $ ^{14} $ This allows the study to keep information awareness costs largely constant in analyzing their information usage patterns.

Another unique institutional feature in HK is the data availability on daily broker-level holdings. Researchers can directly observe the daily aggregate holdings by all FutuBull users for each listed firm. The view tracking feature of the platform, combined with the institutional feature that mandates the disclosure of daily brokerage-level holdings provides a unique opportunity to simultaneously observe both the information usage and trading decisions of retail investors. The setting of the study itself is also important economically, with the HK stock market ranked fifth worldwide by total market capitalization in 2020. Moreover, the HK stock market has relatively wide participation by retail investors, providing a powerful setting to study retail investors. $ ^{15} $

### 3.3 SAMPLE SELECTION

This registered report scrapes the number of views from a total of 1,047,124 regulatory filing and media articles under the corporate news feeds on FutuBull's integrated trading platform for 2579 firms listed on the HKSE between July 1, 2014, and June 30, 2021. Table 1 outlines the sample selection process for the main tables. Data on each broker's daily aggregate holdings for each firm is obtained from the Central Clearing and Settlement System (CCASS) in HK. Stock returns and financial variables used to construct other firm-level characteristics are calculated using the data from Capital IQ.

platform with view tracking features: regulatory filings and media articles. $ ^{16} $ Filings with accounting disclosures are denoted by the Accounting Filings indicator while media articles that cover accounting information identified using a combination of textual analyses on article titles and the timing of release are denoted by the Accounting News indicator. Collectively, Accounting Filings and Accounting News comprise Accounting Documents. Figure 2 outlines different types of documents in the setting.

Accounting Filings include Annual Reports, Semi-Annual Reports, Results Announcements, and Results Guidance. In the HK setting, Results Announcements are filed when firms announce their periodic financial performance. $ ^{17} $ An-

<div style="text-align: center;"><img src="https://pplines-online.bj.bcebos.com/deploy/official/paddleocr/pp-ocr-vl-15//22a905f7-12c5-4823-a9b5-c10bf3e41b54/markdown_0/imgs/img_in_image_box_141_157_793_575.jpg?authorization=bce-auth-v1%2FALTAKzReLNvew3ySINYJ0fuAMN%2F2026-04-04T14%3A58%3A23Z%2F-1%2F%2Fd8d3fce1f5867f710b1efcc84b6ebb21238ebf50c8121b6263b1d81bbded595d" alt="Image" width="67%" /></div>


<div style="text-align: center;"><div style="text-align: center;">Fig. 2.—Documents with Accounting Information in the Setting</div> </div>


nual Reports are analogous to 10-K filings while Semi-Annual Reports are analogous to 10-Q filings in the US setting. $ ^{18} $ Results Guidance is typically filed several weeks before Results Announcements when insiders possess knowledge that the financial performance in the current reporting period significantly deviates either positively or negatively from the comparable period in the previous fiscal year. Firms typically provide rough-range estimates of the upcoming reported revenue and/or earnings in Results Guidance.

Among Accounting News, the study classifies those providing summary highlights of earnings news as Summary Reports based on a combination of word counts (<200 Chinese words) and publisher types. Summary Reports likely have low information extraction costs concerning key accounting items. The study classifies media articles that provide in-depth analyses by sell-side analysts or other information intermediaries as Expert Opinion based on a combination of word counts (>800 Chinese words) and publisher types. $ ^{19} $ Expert Opinion likely has low information integration costs as

retail investors could free-ride on information synthesis by these seemingly more professional parties.

3.4.2. View Counts. The study extracts the cumulative number of views on each document from FutuBull's trading platform (#Views). To mitigate the concerns that some views are generated by internal vetting or other systematic procedures, the study subtracts the minimum view counts on the “monthly return of equity issuer on movement in securities” within the quarter from the raw view counts. These periodic reports summarize the changes in the company's share capital at the end of each month and typically contain no new information to the market. $ ^{20} $ As the distribution is highly right-skewed, the study takes the logarithmic transformations on view counts when used in regressions.

3.4.3. Retail Trading Decisions. The study utilizes the aggregate daily holdings by the clients of FutuBull in each listed firm to examine the trading decisions of retail investors. Changes in aggregate daily holdings by FutuBull's investors within the first two trading days  $ [0, +1] $ after Results Announcements reflect the trading decisions by retail investors after processing earnings news. Changes from one week before earnings announcements to one day before earnings announcements  $ [-7, -1] $ can capture those self-directed retail investors who trade in anticipation of upcoming earnings announcements.

The aggregate daily holdings of I-Access users are used as an example of no-frills trading platforms that provide virtually no information services. Similar to FutuBull, I-Access is an online stockbroker that offers a low-cost trading environment for retail investors. Unlike FutuBull, however, the trading platform of I-Access provides no access to any financial information. $ ^{21} $

Appendix D displays the trends in the investors' holding on these two platforms over time. The pattern suggests that there has been enormous growth in online trading platforms that target retail investors in HK. Besides retail investors that utilize nonintegrated trading platforms, the study also utilizes the aggregate daily holdings by selected Bulge Bracket brokers that primarily serve institutional investors as additional benchmarks for comparison.

3.4.4. Retail Trading Informativeness. Following Kelly and Tetlock [2016], the study examines whether the changes in aggregate holdings by different groups of investors are predictive of future stock returns over a 5-day or 20-day window to shed light on the informativeness of their trading decisions. Additionally, the study examines the aggregate holding changes by FutuBull users around accounting disclosures. Among those events with clear trading directions (measured by having over 3% percentage differences over the window) exhibited by retail investors at FutuBull, the study calculates the trading profitability of these trading decisions assuming a 3-month or 6-month holding period. Higher trading profitability represents more informed trading from processing accounting disclosures.

3.4.5. Investor Attention. Lawrence et al. [2018] find that investor attention affects the trading decisions of retail investors. The study examines several proxies for investor attention. In addition to common proxies such as firm size, returns, trading volume, stock volatility, disclosure timing, and concurrent announcements (#Announcements) (e.g., Barber and Odean [2007]; Hirshleifer, Seongyeon Lim, and Hong Teoh [2009]), the study also proxies for the differences in firm-level awareness costs by the number of firms each article is tagged (#Firms Tagged) or the total number of views in those co-tagged articles in which a given firm is tagged with other firms (Co-Tagged Views) within the two days  $ [0,+1] $ after the release for a given document. The study also uses indicator variables to represent increased article awareness within the platform if the article appears in the headline news feeds (Headlines), is categorized under featured topics on the platform (Featured Topics), or is shared to community discussion forums by the number of shares (Shares).

## 4. Empirical Analyses

### 4.1 PATTERNS OF INFORMATION ACCESS

4.1.1. Total Cumulative Views. Figure 3 illustrates the general information usage patterns by self-directed retail investors on the FutuBull platform. As shown in panel A, while regulatory filings comprise 37.3% of all documents, they only represent 2.8% of total cumulative views. This suggests that these retail investors typically prefer accessing media articles over regulatory filings. Panel B shows that firm-specific articles represent 62.3% of the quantity of media articles but contribute to only 21.6% of cumulative views, indicating that FutuBull users are potentially more inclined to access articles that cover information that is not firm-specific.

Panel C further shows the distribution of views and quantity by publication sources. While 84% of the feeds are sourced from major financial news portals, they only contribute to 23% of total cumulative views. Surprisingly, while only 4% of the feeds are sourced from FutuBull in-house, they contribute almost 66% of total cumulative views. Such a contrast suggests that integrated trading platforms may influence users' information access. Robo-journalism also shows a strong divergence in cumulative views (0.04%) and quantity (1.26%), suggesting that retail investors are less interested in accessing articles created by robo-journalism.

In panel D, media articles / regulatory filings covering accounting information contribute 13%/13.8 of the total quantity but only 5%/8.1% of cumulative views. These differences suggest that retail investors likely regard certain nonaccounting information as more valuable. Panel E fur

Panel F shows that articles promoted in the headline news feeds (representing only 3.3% of the total quantity of media articles) contribute to 62.4% of total cumulative views. This may explain why in-house articles dominate in total cumulative views as they are more likely to be promoted in the headline news feeds. Articles that appear in the thematic news feeds (representing only 1.4% of the total quantity of media articles) contribute to 22.7% of total cumulative views. These patterns are consistent with these retail investors seemingly adopting mostly a top-down approach in their information access through browsing more general market, industry, or thematic information instead of being fixated on the news of specific firms in their watchlists.

In panel A, retail investors appear to display a disproportionately strong interest in accessing the information from selected firms in the middle (fifth) decile and the highest (ninth) decile of market capitalization. In panel B, cumulative views increase monotonically as the decile of economic interests held by FutuBull users increases from 0 (low)

to 9 (high). The evidence is consistent with retail investors rationally allocating their efforts based on economic interests.

4.1.2. Views per Document. Table 2, panel A, shows the information access statistics at the document level. The pattern indicates a preference for accessing filings with more timely accounting information, as evidenced by the higher mean for Results Guidance (159) and lower mean for Semi-Annual Reports (105). The median views over Summary Reports (148) are higher than those of Results Announcements (102), consistent with retail investors preferring shorter documents with lower information processing costs. The median views over Expert Opinion (150) are even slightly higher, consistent with retail investors displaying an interest in documents that help lower their information integration costs. Table 2, panel B, shows that the log-transformed number of view counts (Views) has a mean of 4.7 and median of 4.5, and the distribution is no longer severely right-skewed.

Table 3 compares information access patterns at the document level using regression. The dependent variable is the log-transformed view counts (#views). Along with various indicators on document types, the independent variables include a variety of proxies for title characteristics, investor attention, and firm characteristics, with time and industry/firm-fixed effects. Panel A pools all the documents retrieved under corporate news feeds in the sample. Consistent with figure 3, the results show that retail investors display significantly less interest in regulatory filings (~36% less for Filings), documents covering accounting information (~10% less for Accounting Documents), documents covering firm-specific information (~33%-35% less for Firm Specific), and robo-journalism articles (~43%-53% less for Robo), but significantly more interest in in-house news articles from FutuBull platform (~94%-96% higher for Platform).

Interestingly, retail investors display significantly less interest in documents containing brokerage research information ( $ \sim $11%-17% less for Broker Research) but more interest in crowdsourced content from financial bloggers/influencers ( $ \sim $23%-31% higher for Crowdsourced). The difference suggests that retail investors appear to prefer crowdsourced content over traditional equity research in which professional investors are the target audience. One potential explanation is that crowdsourced content may have lower information integration costs as they are likely the target audience. Another potential explanation is that some users use the platform not only as a trading venue but also as a news reading app.

On the FutuBull platform, retail investors can observe the title of each document under corporate news feeds before deciding to access it through clicks. The results show that the length of the article title (Title Length) exhibits a strong negative association with Views, consistent with having long informative titles reducing the need to read the detailed content inside. Retail investors also display significantly less interest in titles with strong positive sentiment (Positive), but higher interest in titles containing numeric information (Numeric).

Panel A displays the summary viewership statistics on different types of documents among the entire set of 1,047,124 documents. Panel B displays the descriptive statistics of the main variables used in the sample for various tables. See appendix A for variable definitions.

Various proxies for investor attention are highly significant in the regression. Consistent with figure 3, retail investors display significantly higher interest in articles that appear in thematic news feeds ( $ \sim $74%–88% higher for Featured Topics) and headline news feeds ( $ \sim $81%–88% higher for Highlight), suggesting that retail investor attention appear to be easily drawn to what is being promoted on the platform. Views also exhibit a strong positive association with the number of corporate news feeds the article appears in (t-statistics: >37 for #Firms Tagged) and the number of retail investors sharing the article within the FutuBull community platform (t-statistics: >22 for Shares). Views are significantly higher when there is higher stock price volatility ( $ |Return| $) and trading volume (Turnover) on the day the article is released, but lower if the article is released after the trading hour ( $ \sim $15%–19% lower for After-Hour), providing indirect evidence that their information acquisition mostly happens during trading and they generally access the timeliest information available. Consistent with the attention dilution effect, Views are lower if the number of articles available under news feeds

This table displays the descriptive analyses examining the type of information investors display an interest in viewing on the integrated trading platform and the factors associated with their viewing patterns. Panel A uses all media articles and regulatory filings on the platform as the sample for analyses. Panel B focuses on media articles that contain firm-specific information as the sample for analyses. Panel C focuses on regulatory filings as the sample for analyses. See appendix A for variable definitions. The standard errors are clustered by firm. The t-statistics are in parentheses.  $ ^{***} $,  $ ^{**} $, and  $ ^{*} $ indicate significance at the 0.01, 0.05, and 0.10 levels, respectively.

(#Articles) or the number of results-related announcements made by all firms (#Announcements) increases on the same day the article is released.

In terms of firm-level characteristics, the only significant variables are firm size (Size) and the percentage of stakes held by FutuBull investors (Stake) in column (2) with firm-fixed effects. The two variables both exhibit a strong positive association with Views and comprise the components in calculating economic interests displayed in figure 4, panel B.

In panel B, the study focuses on articles that cover firm-specific information as the regression sample. The results are largely consistent with panel A, except that retail investors display a higher interest in accessing articles that cover accounting information ( $ \sim $5% higher for Accounting News) and brokerage research information ( $ \sim $5% higher for Broker Research) in this sample using other firm-specific articles as the benchmarks. Retail investors also display a higher interest in accessing articles with strong negative sentiment ( $ \sim $6% higher for Negative) and positive sentiment ( $ \sim $4% higher for Positive) than those with neutral sentiment. The coefficient on Co-Tagged Views that aggregates the total views of other contemporaneous articles co-tagged with other firms is significantly positive, consistent with the presence of spillover attention from other related firms.

Panel C displays the regression results using a sample that contains only regulatory filings. The coefficient on Accounting Filings is significantly negative, suggesting that retail investors display approximately 13% less interest

in accessing filings containing accounting information. Consistent with P1, the coefficient on Results Guidance  $ (-0.055) $ is higher than Results Announcements  $ (-0.105) $. However, the coefficients on Financial Reports  $ (-0.105) $ and Results Announcements  $ (-0.106) $ are similar in magnitude, which is inconsistent with P2.

4.1.3. Accounting Disclosures. Table 4 focuses on the channels through which retail investors access timely accounting information. Panel A conducts document-level analyses of information access patterns among all filings and articles that cover accounting information. In this specification, the coefficients on Results Guidance (−0.33) are significantly lower than Results Announcements (−0.03) after controlling for title characteristics, suggesting that retail investors are perhaps only more reactive to the titles with more salient sentiments in Results Guidance. Across all three columns, the coefficients on Results Announcements are significantly higher than those for Financial Reports (consistent with P2) and Summary Reports. $ ^{92} $ The results in column (2) suggest that retail investors also display a strong interest in accessing articles providing analyses by nonbrokerage sources, as evidenced by a large positive coefficient on Expert Opinions (0.28) but a large negative coefficient on Brokerage Research (−0.54), where Brokerage Research is a subset of Expert Opinions. Column (3) further breaks down Brokerage Research into those providing the full text from Chinese brokerage houses that typically provide the reports for free (Broker Research Report) and those providing summary ratings from international brokerage houses (Broker Summary Ratings). The results show that retail investors exhibit much lower interest in Broker Research Report (−0.61) vs. Broker Summary Ratings (−0.26).

Panel B conducts event-level analyses on information access patterns over filings and media articles released within two days around earnings announcements. Within this short window around earnings announcements, different documents should have similar information awareness costs as they appear in chronological order under the same news feeds. $ ^{23} $ This allows the analyses to elicit investors' information choice after holding the underlying information event constant as well as the awareness costs relatively similar. As there are potentially multiple documents from the same type in this short window, information access metrics are aggregated by document types using mean, maximum, and sum for each earnings announcement. Columns (1) to (3) require the presence of both Results Announcements and Summary Reports in the event window while columns (4) to (6)

This table presents the regression results examining how investors on FutuBull access timely accounting disclosures. See appendix A for variable definitions. The standard errors are clustered by firm. The t-statistics are in parentheses. ***, **, and * indicate significance at the 0.01, 0.05, and 0.10 levels, respectively.

require the presence of all three types Results Announcements, Summary Reports, and Expert Opinions in the event window.

Consistent with both P3 and P4, the results show that retail investors indeed display a higher interest in accessing news articles providing short summaries or in-depth analyses than Result Announcement filings around earnings announcements, as evidenced by the significantly positive Summary Reports and Expert Opinions across all columns. This finding is consistent with some retail investors seeking to lower their information acquisition and integration costs from these types of articles. $ ^{24} $ Using earnings surprises (Surprise) and accruals (Accrual) as proxies for information complexities, the results also show that retail investors are more likely to access the detailed filings on Results Announcement when the announcement involves a larger magnitude of earnings surprises or accruals in the sample of columns (1) to (3).

Panel C examines whether information intermediaries facilitate or substitute the direct access of accounting disclosures from corporate filings, utilizing the plausibly exogenous introduction of major new information sources to FutuBull's corporate news feeds over time. If the newly added information source does not uniformly cover all listed firms, there will be variations in “treated” and “non-treated” firms for each “event.” The study includes up to two years of observations before and after each “event” to conduct difference-in-difference analyses as specified in equation (1).

 $$ V i e w s\left(F i l i n g s\right)=\beta_{1}P o s t+\beta_{3}P o s t*T r e a t e d+\beta_{x}C o n t r o l s\&F.E.+\varepsilon.\left(1\right) $$ 

The study identifies four clusters of such events, including the introduction of new financial news portals in column (1), new newsflash sources in column (2), new in-house information sources in column (3), and new brokerage research sources in column (4). The results show that  $ Post*Treated $ are insignificant across four columns, failing to find any evidence supporting either a complementary  $ (P5a) $ or substitutive  $ (P5b) $ relationship in the information access between information intermediaries and regulatory filings.

### 4.2 INTEGRATING ACCOUNTING INFORMATION IN TRADING DECISIONS

4.2.1. Trading Decision Around Earnings Announcements. In testing the hypotheses, this registered report assesses to what extent self-directed retail investors on integrated trading platforms manage to integrate accounting information into their trading decisions. Table 5 examines whether retail investors on FutuBull trade in the same direction as the news content during earnings announcements, similar in spirit to Hirshleifer et al. [2008].

The basic model setup is described in equation (2). The dependent variable is the change in aggregate holdings of  $ FutuBull's $ clients around  $ Results Announcements. $^{25} The major independent variable is the news contained in  $ Results Announcements $ proxied by the firm's market-adjusted returns in the announcement window ( $ News $).^{26}

 $$ \Delta H o l d i n g_{t}=\beta_{1}N e w s+\beta_{x}C o n t r o l s~a n d~F.E.+\varepsilon. $$ 

The results indicate that News is insignificant across all measurement windows of  $ \Delta\text{Holding} $:  $ [-7\text{d},+1\text{d}] $ in column (1),  $ [-7\text{d},-1\text{d}] $ in column (2),  $ [0,+1\text{d}] $ in column (3), suggesting that retail investors are not saliently trading in the same direction of earnings news both before and during earnings announcements on average using the platform. Columns (4)–(6) further examine whether a high level of information access on different types of accounting documents is associated with retail trades during the earnings announcements window  $ \Delta\text{Holding} $  $ [0,+1\text{d}] $ as described in equation (3).

 $$ \begin{aligned}\Delta Holding_{t}&=\beta_{1}News+\beta_{2}High Views_{i}+\beta_{3}News*High Views_{i}\\&+\beta_{x}Controls and F.E.+\varepsilon.\end{aligned} $$ 

In column (4), News is significantly negative (-0.16), while News*High Views (Results Announcements) is significantly positive (+0.31). In other words, retail investors are on average trading in the opposite direction of news during earnings announcements when retail access to Results Announcements filings is low. When retail access to Results Announcements filing is high, retail investors are trading more in line with the news during earnings announcements and potentially more in line with PEAD trading strategies. These findings suggest that there are potentially large heterogeneities in the trading decision by retail investors depending on the level of information access on Results Announcements filings during announcements.

Additionally, High Views (Results Announcements) and High Views (Summary Reports) are both significant and positive, with large t-statistics of 9.33 and 2.52, respectively. As most retail investors likely hold cash accounts with short-sale constraints, the strong positive relationship between holding changes and information access on Results Announcements is consistent with information-driven buying decisions during earnings announcements by these investors. However, whether retail investors can generate extra returns from accessing the information is still unclear.

4.2.2. Informativeness of Retail Trades. Following Kelly and Tetlock [2016], table 6 examines the general informativeness of self-directed retail investors at FutuBull using daily Fama and MacBeth [1973] regression of future cumulative market-adjusted returns measured over either the 5-day (panel A) or 20-day (panel B) window on the daily holding changes by FutuBull.

As a comparison, the regression also includes the holding changes of self-directed retail investors using a no-fills trading platform (l-Access) and the holding changes of investors using selected Bulge Bracket brokers that primarily serve institutions (Bulge Bracket) in columns (3) and (4). Panel A examines the predictability of returns from days t-1 to t+5, while panel B examines the predictability of returns from day t+1 through t+20. The table reports average regression coefficients. Newey and West [11987] t-statistics with four lags appear in parentheses.</td></tr></table>

clients. The model specifications are described in equations (4) and (5). The specification also examines the informativeness of their trades, particularly around accounting disclosures, and compares the informativeness of FutuBull clients against those of I-Access and Bulge Bracket brokers.

 $$ Ret_{t+1,t+d}=\alpha+\beta_{1}\sum_{j}\Delta H olding_{t,j}+\beta_{x}Controls+\varepsilon. $$ 

 $$ \begin{aligned}Ret_{t+1,t+d}&=\alpha+\beta_{1}\sum_{j}\Delta Holding_{t,j}+\beta_{2}Filings(Acc)_{t}\\&+\beta_{3}\sum_{j}\Delta Holding_{t,j}\times Filings(Acc)_{it}\\&+\beta_{4}Filings(Acc)_{t+1,t+5}+\beta_{5}\sum_{j}\Delta Holding_{t,j}\\&\times Filings(Acc)_{t+1,t+5}+\beta_{x}Controls+\varepsilon.\\ \end{aligned} $$ 

Panel A examines the predictability of future five-day returns. The results show that  $ \Delta\text{Holding}(\text{FutuBull}) $ is positive (0.001) with a t-statistic of 1.57 in column (1) and becomes marginally significant with a t-statistic of 1.89 in column (3) when put in a horserace with  $ \Delta\text{Holding}(\text{IAccess}) $ and  $ \Delta\text{Holding}(\text{Bulge Bracket}) $. As a comparison,  $ \Delta\text{Holding}(\text{IAccess}) $ is significantly negative (-0.001) with a t-statistic of -3.76, suggesting that the clients of a no-frills trading platform that provides no accounting information access are generally uninformed traders. These findings suggest that retail investors using integrated trading platforms  $ \Delta\text{FutuBull} $ are more informed traders relative to those using I-Access.  $ \Delta\text{Holding}(\text{Bulge Bracket}) $ is also positive (0.001) with a t-statistic of 1.54 in column (3), displaying no significant differences from  $ \Delta\text{Holding}(\text{FutuBull}) $. This suggests that retail investors using the  $ \Delta\text{FutuBull} $ platform are as informed as institutional investors that utilize  $ \Delta\text{Bulge Bracket} $ brokers in the sample.

When  $ \Delta\text{Holding}(FutuBull) $ interacts with an indicator  $ \text{Filings}(Acc)_{[-1d,+5d]} $ for having upcoming accounting-related disclosures in the next five days as well as an indicator  $ \text{Filings}(Acc)_{[-1d,0]} $ for having accounting-related disclosures on day t or t-1 in column (2), the interaction term between  $ \Delta\text{Holding}(FutuBull) $ and  $ \text{Filings}(Acc)_{[-1d,0]} $ is significantly positive with a t-statistics of 1.99. This result shows that  $ \text{FutuBull} $ users are incrementally more informative in their equity trades in the [0, +1 day] window around earnings announcements, providing evidence supporting the notion that more convenient access to timely accounting information facilitates more informed trading by retail investors.

Panel B replicates the analyses in panel A by expanding the return predictability horizon from 5 days to 20 days. The results are largely consistent in both panels, with  $ \Delta\text{Holding}(FutuBull) $ being significant across all four columns with t-statistics of at least 3.36 in panel B. Under this measurement horizon, retail investors at the FutuBull platform continue to behave significantly more informed than those at I-Access and stay as informed as institutional investors that utilize Bulge Bracket brokers. Collectively, the evidence in table 6 supports the alternative form of H1.

4.2.3. Trading Profitability and Information Access. Table 7 further examines the potential trading profitability of FutuBull clients and its association with different channels of accounting information access. The sample is limited to Results Announcements that contain value-relevant information proxied by over 5% market-adjusted stock price movement during the announcement window and those with clear trading direction exhibited by FutuBull users (“net buy” if  $ \Delta $Holdings is over +3% and “net sell” if  $ \Delta $Holdings is below -3%). $ ^{27} $ Trading Profitability is thus calculated depending on the trading direction of FutuBull clients in these events. Equation (6) describes the regression specification. The dependent variable Trading Profitability represents the cumulative market-adjusted return from these trades in a 3-month or a 6-month horizon. Key independent variables include the information access on Results Announcements, Summary Reports, and Expert Opinion, proxied by the sum of views within each document type scaled by the dollar amount of economic interests held by FutuBull clients in these firms. $ ^{28} $

Trade Profitability =  $ \beta_{\mathrm{x}} $ Scaled Views +  $ \beta_{\mathrm{y}} $ Controls and F.E. +  $ \varepsilon $.

The results generally find positive but insignificant coefficients on the Sum Scaled Views (Results Announcements), with t-statistics of 1.10 in column (1) and 1.35 in column (5). Sum Scaled Views (Summary Reports) is insignificantly positive, while Sum Scaled Views (Expert Opinion) is insignificantly negative. While statistically insignificant (potentially due to measurement errors), the direction of the association between aggregative view counts on Results Announcements and trade profitability is consistent with the notion that accessing detailed accounting information in regulatory filings may provide benefits to retail investors. $ ^{29} $

4.2.4. Comparisons Across Trading Platforms. Table 8 further compares the trading decisions and profitability of self-directed retail investors at FutuBull with those at I-Access and Bulge Bracket brokers when there are accounting-related disclosures (Results Announcements, Financial Reports, and Results Guidance). Panel A utilizes equation (7) in examining whether users at the two platforms are incorporating accounting information differently in their trading decisions.

 $$ \begin{aligned}\Delta Holding&=\beta_{1}News+\beta_{2}News*I-Access/Bulge\\&+\beta_{x}Controls and F.E.+\varepsilon.\end{aligned} $$ 

The results show that News is insignificantly positive (t-statistics of 1.19 and 1.31) in columns (1) and (3) when  $ \Delta\text{Holding}_{[5,1]} $ is used as the dependent variables, suggesting that some FutuBull traders may be anticipating upcoming news in their trades despite the statistical insignificance. In columns (2) and (4) when  $ \Delta\text{Holding}_{[0,1]} $ is used as the dependent variables, News becomes significantly negative (t-statistics of -2.83 and -3.00), suggesting that retail investors at FutuBull are on average contrarian traders when there are accounting disclosures. The results combined are consistent with some retail investors at FutuBull potentially trading in anticipation of upcoming news in accounting disclosures and unwinding their positions upon news release. However, this does not appear to be a salient and pervasive strategy. The findings also suggest that retail investors do not generally incorporate accounting information following PEAD strategies.

Panel B compares the profitability of trades initiated in the  $ [-5d, -1d] $ window before earnings announcements in anticipation of upcoming accounting disclosures between FutuBull and I-Access/Bulge Bracket, conditional on having a clear trading direction exhibited by platform users (i.e.,  $ |\Delta\text{Holding}| > 3\% $). The profitability is measured as the cumulative market-adjusted returns in the  $ [0, +1d] $ or  $ [0, +7d] $ announcement window. Equation (8) describes the specification.

Trade Profitability $ _{t} = \beta_{1}Futu + \beta_{x}Controls $ and F.E.+ $ \varepsilon $.

This table presents the regression results comparing the trading directions and profitability between retail investors using FutuBull vs. I-Access as well as between retail investors using FutuBull vs. institutional investors using Bulge Bracket brokers. Across the panels, columns (1) and (2) compare between the retail investors using FutuBull and I-Access, whereas columns (3) and (4) compare between the retail investors using FutuBull and institutional investors using Bulge Bracket platforms. See appendix A for variable definitions. The standard errors are clustered by firm. The t-statistics are in parentheses.  $ ^{***} $,  $ ^{**} $, and  $ ^{*} $ indicate significance at the 0.01, 0.05, and 0.10 levels, respectively.

The results show that Futu is insignificantly positive in columns (1) and (2) with t-statistics of 0.31 and 1.05 when compared with I-Access clients as the omitted category, but insignificantly negative in columns (3) and (4) with t-statistics of -0.92 and -0.17 when compared with investors using Bulge Bracket brokers. Overall, there is no strong evidence suggesting that FutuBull clients are significantly better at anticipating future accounting disclosures than the benchmarks.

Panel C further compares the profitability of trades conducted in the  $ [0, +1d] $ window after accounting for information release, assuming a holding period of either three or six months, conditional on the announcements involving a clear trading direction exhibited by platform users (i.e.,  $ |\Delta Holding|>3\% $). The results reveal significant positive coefficients on Futu (t-statistics: 2.74 and 2.78) in columns (1) and (2) when FutuBull is compared with I-Access, suggesting that the trading profitability from processing accounting disclosures is 1.2% higher in the 3-month window and 1.9% higher in the 6-month window for FutuBull users vs. I-Access users. In columns (3) and (4), Futu is insignificantly positive (t-statistics: 0.31 and 0.82) when FutuBull is compared against Bulge Bracket brokers, revealing no significant differences in the ability to process accounting disclosures between FutuBull and Bulge Bracket clients.

This table explores to what extent investors at different trading/brokerage platforms take advantage of post-earnings-announcement drift (PEAD) opportunities after financial results announcements. The table compares the performance of investors using different platforms in capturing PEAD opportunities (either in a three-month or six-month window) by tabulating how many times the aggregate trading behavior of users at these platforms (#Futu, #I-Access, #Bulge Bracket) is consistent with the PEAD opportunities during earnings announcements as well as the aggregate directional bets by platform users during earnings announcements under these circumstances (ΔStake%).

The evidence presented in panels B and C is consistent with the empirical evidence presented in table 6 using Fama-MacBeth regression using a shorter window of five and 20 days. The collective evidence suggests that having more convenient access to corporate information facilitates more profitable trades by self-directed retail investors after processing earnings news, providing strong evidence supporting the alternative form of  $ H_{2} $.

### 4.3 ADDITIONAL ANALYSES

4.3.1. Capturing PEAD Opportunities. Table 9 compares how often investors from the three types of brokers capture the significant PEAD opportunities that generate 5% or 10% excess returns over a three-month or six-month horizon by analyzing their aggregate trading directions during earning announcements. The table does not show much difference between the number of times FutuBull and I-Access clients manage to capture the PEAD opportunities, suggesting that retail investors at FutuBull are not systematically exploiting potential PEAD anomalies in their trading decisions.

This table presents the regression results examining the association between viewership on accounting-related disclosures through various channels and the stock's intraperiod efficiency (IPE). The sample is limited to those Results Announcements that contain value-relevant information as proxied by generating over 5% of market-adjusted stock price movement in the two-day window  $ [0,+5] $ after their releases as well as firms having over 3% average aggregate holding by Futubull users around earnings announcements. The dependent variables are an area-under-curve measure of the speed of price adjustment within the  $ [0,+5] $ window after earnings announcement IPE $ [0,+5] $. See appendix A for variable definitions. The standard errors are clustered by firm. The t-statistics are in parentheses.  $ ^{***} $,  $ ^{**} $, and  $ ^{*} $ indicate significance at the 0.01, 0.05, and 0.10 levels, respectively. # represents variables that are automatically dropped in the estimation process due to the lack of variations or collinearity with other variables/fixed effects.

4.3.2. Price Efficiency. Kelley and Tetlok [2016] find that retail short sellers have unique insights into small firms' fundamentals. Table 10 examines whether greater access to timely accounting information by self-directed retail investors contributes to higher intraperiod price efficiency in small-cap stocks. $ ^{30} $ Potentially due to the small sample size, the results find an insignificant positive coefficient on  $ |\Delta\text{Holding(FutuBull)}| $ with a t-statistic of

1.53 and an insignificant positive coefficient on  $ |\Delta\text{Holding}(FutuBull)| \times \text{High Views}(Results\ \text{Announcements}) $ with a t-statistic of 1.50, providing some preliminary evidence that accounting information access and trading by retail investors may enhance price efficiency in small-cap stocks.

## 5. Concluding Remarks

Through analyzing data from an integrated trading platform, this registered report provides a more holistic picture of the information access patterns of self-directed retail investors. The results show that these retail investors access a mosaic of information, with a significant portion being not firm-specific. Investor attention appears to be the most salient determinant. While only 3.3% of media articles are promoted in the headline, they contribute to 62.4% of total cumulative views of media articles, suggesting that trading platforms can potentially exert a strong influence on the information access pattern of users.

Among articles that provide firm-specific coverage, retail investors display a higher interest in accessing those covering accounting information, supporting the importance of accounting information as a source of firm-specific information for retail investors. During earnings announcements, retail investors typically prefer accessing articles providing summary reports or in-depth analyses from reputable sources relative to the original Results Announcement filings. This is consistent with P3 and P4, suggesting that disclosure processing costs present frictions to the direct use of accounting information from regulatory filings for retail investors.

With convenient access to a rich set of information through the integrated trading platform, retail trades at FutuBull are generally predictive of future stock returns, exhibiting significantly higher informativeness than I-Access, a no-frills trading platform that provides no information services, and demonstrating no significant differences in informativeness compared with institutional trades at Bulge Bracket brokers. These findings suggest that retail investors benefit significantly from having convenient access to a mosaic of information in the trading environment and can narrow the information gap relative to institutional investors.

In particular, the informativeness of retail trades at FutuBull is more pronounced just after accounting disclosures, suggesting that the sources of informed trading partly come from processing accounting disclosures. During earnings announcements, aggregate retail trades at FutuBull tend to be contrarian if the level of their access to Results Announcement filing is low. Aggregate retail trades become more in line with earnings news if their access to Results Announcement filing is high. This suggests that the trading strategies by self-directed retail investors are likely quite heterogeneous and do not follow PEAD trading strategies systematically.

Overall, the collective evidence supports the alternative form of both  $ H_{1} $ and  $ H_{2} $ in the registered proposal, but this registered report cannot clearly identify which specific pieces of information contribute to informed trading by retail investors in accounting disclosures. The study finds an insignificant positive relationship between trading profitability and access to Results Announcement filings when linking retail trading to information access within the platform. It is possible that informed trading comes from having access to a mosaic of information that completes the picture, not just from a single piece of information in isolation on the integrated trading platform.

There are several limitations and caveats in this registered report. First, the study does not observe the amount of time retail investors spent reading and analyzing the information contained in each document on the platform. Therefore, not all views necessarily represent retail investors' diligent efforts in information extraction and integration. Second, the study only observes the aggregate viewership at the document level and the net trading behavior of "representative" retail investors at the firm level instead of tracking both at the individual level. Measurement errors due to data constraints may introduce noise or unknown bias in the test linking retail trades to information access within the platform. Nonsignificance in the results may be due to reduced test power from such measurement errors. Third, the study cannot disentangle whether the differences across platforms are attributable to client matching based on information demand or differences induced by differential information costs. Nevertheless, the findings can still speak to the heterogeneity of retail investors and the importance of information costs for self-directed retail investors. Fourth, the study cannot completely rule out the possibility that differences in trading decisions and profitability may be explained by other omitted factors correlated with investors' usage of information during the underlying events. Last, the findings can speak to self-directed retail investors who trade through online/mobile apps and may not generalize to other retail investors who rely on broker advisory services in investment decisions.
